If I get caught up checking or rechecking things before I leave, then everything takes longer than it should, and I end up arriving at school feeling rushed, like I’m already behind before the students are even there or before the day has really started. And that feeling can stick with me for a while, even if technically I’m on time and nothing has gone wrong.\n\nLesson prep is probably the other part. I’m still preparing, I’m still getting things done, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. It can feel more drawn out, like I have to spend extra mental energy making sure I didn’t miss something or that everything is set up properly. So I can still perform my job, but it feels harder than it should, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I’m more worn out, and even my evening journaling, which used to help me reset, doesn’t feel as restorative. So it’s not like I’m failing at teaching, but the whole process around work feels more stressful and less efficient than it used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are making it difficult for you to concentrate or be fully present when you're interacting with your students or colleagues?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 31 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still getting through work and family commitments, but my mornings take longer and I sometimes arrive at school feeling rushed or behind before the day even starts. Lesson prep and my evening journaling don’t feel as smooth or restorative as they used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it does, at least sometimes. Not to the point where I can’t teach or I’m not able to interact with people, but there are moments where I’m physically there and doing what I need to do, and mentally there’s still this leftover feeling from the morning, like I’m trying to catch up with myself.

If I’ve spent too long checking things before leaving, then I get to school feeling rushed or already behind, even if I’m technically there on time. And that makes it harder to feel calm and fully settled when I’m starting the day with the students or talking with colleagues. I can still answer questions and teach and do the normal things, but it feels like I’m using more effort to focus than I should be.

Lesson prep is similar. I still get it done, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. I’ll be working through things and it’s like part of my attention is still caught up in making sure I didn’t miss something, so I don’t feel as efficient or as present as I used to.

And then by the evening, I notice I’m more mentally tired. Even journaling, which used to help me unwind and feel restored, doesn’t really do that as much lately. So yes, I’d say it affects my concentration and presence, but mildly. I’m still getting through work and family responsibilities — it just takes more out of me than it used to.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I keep getting stuck on these thoughts that I’ve forgotten something important, even when part of me knows I probably didn’t. Like, I’ll think maybe I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or I didn’t put something away safely in my classroom materials, and then the thought just keeps coming back.\n\nI’m a teacher, so I’m used to being careful and organized, and I know things matter when kids are involved, but this feels different. I’ll try to tell myself, “No, you did that,” but then it’s like, “But what if you didn’t?” And I can’t quite shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over, like checking or cleaning, to try and get rid of these nagging thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Once I’ve gone through it enough, I calm down enough to leave, but sometimes that means I’m running late, which just makes me feel embarrassed and frustrated with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or tense in situations where you're not performing these checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s like the anxiety builds more when I’m trying not to do the checking, if that makes sense. I’ll be about to leave and then I start wondering if I put my lesson plans or materials away properly, and even if I’m not physically touching everything again, I’m sort of mentally retracing it—like, “I did this, then I closed that, then I checked that.” I’ll stand there doing that until I feel calm enough to move on.\n\nIt’s not that I want to be late or that I think checking is helpful in the long run. Sometimes I’m aware I’m making myself run late, and that makes me feel embarrassed. I don’t really have, um, totally separate thoughts that I can think of right now.\n\nIt’s more like, I’ll be pretty sure I handled something, like my aunt’s medicine or setting up things in my classroom, but then the doubt pops back in anyway. Like, “What if I left it out wrong?” or “What if the materials weren’t put somewhere safe?” And even when part of me knows I probably did it correctly, the thought doesn’t just go away. It sort of keeps tapping me on the shoulder, if that makes sense, until I go back over it in my mind or check again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your overall well-being or daily life due to these recurring thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I keep wanting to say it’s “not that bad” because I’m still functioning. I don’t know if I’d call that “a lot of trouble,” exactly, but it does bother me because those little routines used to help me feel more balanced. So I guess it’s not stopping my life, but it’s making normal things feel heavier and less smooth.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social or leisure activities that you've stopped doing or are doing less often because of these thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve fully stopped doing social things, no. Like, I’m not canceling plans all the time or avoiding people in a really obvious way. It’s not that I never enjoy anything, but there’s this extra layer of tension or mental noise over normal daily routines.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s interfering with my enjoyment of daily life. Mildly, maybe, but enough that I notice it and it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are affecting your ability to perform your job as a teacher?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, but not in a dramatic way. Once I’ve gone through it enough, I calm down enough to leave, but sometimes that means I’m running late, which just makes me feel embarrassed and frustrated with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or tense in situations where you're not performing these checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Before I leave the house, I can tell myself, “You already looked at the stove,” or “You already checked the pill organizer,” but then I’ll still feel this tight, uneasy feeling, like I can’t fully trust that it’s done unless I go back over it again.\n\nAnd it happens with my classroom too. I’ll be about to leave and then I start wondering if I put my lesson plans or materials away properly, and even if I’m not physically touching everything again, I’m sort of mentally retracing it—like, “I did this, then I closed that, then I checked that.” I’ll stand there doing that until I feel calm enough to move on.\n\nIt’s not that I want to be late or that I think checking is helpful in the long run. Sometimes I’m aware I’m making myself run late, and that makes me feel embarrassed. But in the moment, if I don’t check or replay it a few times, I feel too tense to just leave.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and checking rituals affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think. Like, I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do—I go to work, I help with family things, I show up for people. So from the outside it may not look like it’s really affecting anything.\n\nBut it does sort of spill over. My mornings take longer than they should because I get stuck checking before I leave, and then I’ll get to school feeling rushed, like I’m already behind before the day even starts. And that can make me a little short or distracted, not because I’m upset with anyone, but because I’m trying to catch up mentally.\n\nWith family, I still keep up with things, but I feel like I’m not as relaxed as I used to be. Even my lesson prep, or journaling in the evening—which used to help me unwind—doesn’t feel as smooth or calming anymore. It’s like there’s this extra layer of tension around getting things “right” or making sure I didn’t miss anything. So relationships haven’t fallen apart or anything, but I can feel it taking up space in my day and affecting how present I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that aren't related to checking or making sure things are done correctly?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s around that same kind of theme, I think—like these nagging thoughts that I forgot something important or didn’t do something safely. I don’t really have, um, totally separate thoughts that I can think of right now.\n\nIt’s more like, I’ll be pretty sure I handled something, like my aunt’s medicine or setting up things in my classroom, but then the doubt pops back in anyway. Like, “What if I left it out wrong?” or “What if the materials weren’t put somewhere safe?” And even when part of me knows I probably did it correctly, the thought doesn’t just go away. It sort of keeps tapping me on the shoulder, if that makes sense, until I go back over it in my mind or check again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your overall well-being or daily life due to these recurring thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I keep wanting to say it’s “not that bad” because I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, I’m still handling family commitments, I’m not missing major responsibilities or anything like that. But it does affect the rhythm of my day more than I’d like to admit.\n\nThe mornings are probably the clearest example. I’ll think I’m ready to leave, and then the checking or the mental reviewing adds these extra minutes that don’t seem like much at first, but they stack up. So by the time I actually get to school, I can feel rushed, like I’m already starting the day behind. And that feeling kind of follows me into the classroom sometimes, even if I’m trying to act normal and get right into teacher mode.\n\nIt’s also made things that used to feel steady or calming feel less smooth. Lesson prep used to be something I could sit down and do in a pretty focused way, but now there’s more tension around whether I’ve done it correctly or put everything where it needs to go. And in the evenings, I journal, which used to help me unwind, but even that doesn’t feel as restorative lately. I’ll still do it, but I don’t always get that same settled feeling from it.\n\nSo I guess overall, I’m managing, but it takes more effort than it used to. It’s like there’s this extra layer of stress built into ordinary parts of the day.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are causing you significant distress or impairment?\n\nPatient: I think the biggest areas are mornings and work, mostly. I wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to function, because I’m not—I do get to work, I do teach, I still handle family commitments. So part of me feels like I shouldn’t call it “significant,” but it is distressing in this quieter, daily kind of way.\n\nLike in the mornings, leaving the house takes longer than it really should. I’ll be basically ready, and then I get pulled into checking or mentally going back over things, and by the time I actually leave, I’m later than I meant to be. Then I get to school feeling rushed or like I’m already behind before the day even starts, which is frustrating because nothing has technically gone wrong yet, but I already feel tense.\n\nAnd lesson prep has changed too. It used to feel more straightforward—like I could sit down, plan, organize things, and feel done. Now it doesn’t feel as smooth, because there’s this extra worry about whether everything is right or whether I missed something. Even my evening journaling, which used to be kind of calming for me, doesn’t feel as restorative as it used to. I still do it, but it doesn’t always help me settle in the same way.\n\nSo yes, I guess it is causing distress and some impairment, just not in a dramatic “my life is falling apart” way. More like it makes ordinary parts of the day take more energy than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of these nagging thoughts or checking rituals?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I really avoid things, not in a clear way. I mean, I still do the things I need to do—I go to work, I get lesson materials ready, I help with family stuff, I leave the house even if it takes me longer than I want. So I wouldn’t say I’m, like, staying away from whole situations because of it.\n\nMaybe there are moments where I feel tempted to put something off because I know it might turn into this whole checking loop, but I usually still do it. It’s more that I move through it with extra hesitation or stress, if that makes sense, rather than avoiding it completely.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure what would count as avoidance. Like, if I take longer to start something because I’m bracing myself for the checking, is that avoidance? Maybe a little? But I haven’t noticed myself outright not doing important tasks or refusing certain situations because of the thoughts. It’s more that I do them, but they feel heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and checking rituals caused problems in your relationships with friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not with friends specifically. At least, I haven’t had anyone say, like, “You’re being distant” or “You’re acting different,” and I’m not aware of any friendships being damaged because of it.\n\nI guess sometimes I might be a little distracted or preoccupied in my own head, but I can’t say that’s caused an actual problem between me and a friend. It’s more something I experience internally, if that makes sense. Like I might feel a little tense or not fully relaxed, but outwardly I’m still showing up and talking to people normally.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really noticed relationship problems with friends because of it. If anything, it’s more just that I’m carrying extra stress around, but I don’t think it’s spilled over into friendships in a major way.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and checking rituals caused problems in your relationships with family members?\n\nPatient: With family, I wouldn’t say it’s caused major conflict or anything like that. I’m still doing the family things I need to do, and I don’t think anyone would say I’ve stopped showing up for them.\n\nBut I do think it affects the “flow” of the day, if that makes sense. Like mornings can take me longer than they should, and if there’s something family-related mixed into that—getting out the door on time, responding to something, helping with whatever needs to happen—I can feel rushed and tense before I’ve even really started the day. Then I arrive at school already feeling behind, and that stress sort of carries over.\n\nI guess at home it’s more that I’m not as relaxed as I want to be. I still keep up with commitments, but I’m using more energy to get through ordinary things. Even in the evenings, journaling used to help me settle down and feel more like myself, and lately it doesn’t feel as smooth or calming. So I wouldn’t say my family relationships are damaged, exactly, but I do think they might be getting a slightly more stressed version of me than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other parts of your life, besides work and family, where you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are causing you a lot of trouble or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… besides work and family, I don’t know that there’s a huge separate area where it’s making me unable to do things. It’s more like it kind of seeps into the parts of the day around everything.\n\nLike mornings are probably the biggest one. I still get up, I still leave, I still get to school, but it takes longer than it should, and then I’ll get there already feeling like I’m behind, even if technically I’m on time or only a little rushed. It puts me in this keyed-up state before the day has even really started.\n\nAnd then with lesson prep, I’m still doing it, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. I might second-guess things or take extra time getting settled into it, so it feels less efficient and more draining than it needs to be.\n\nThe other thing is my evening journaling. That used to be a really calming part of my routine, like a way to end the day and feel organized in my head. Lately it still happens sometimes, but it doesn’t feel as restorative or easy. I don’t know if I’d call that “a lot of trouble,” exactly, but it does bother me because those little routines used to help me feel more balanced. So I guess it’s not stopping my life, but it’s making normal things feel heavier and less smooth.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social or leisure activities that you've stopped doing or are doing less often because of these thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve fully stopped doing social things, no. Like, I’m not canceling plans all the time or avoiding people in a really obvious way. If I have something I’m supposed to do with friends or family, I usually still do it.\n\nBut I do think some of the “leisure” parts of my day don’t feel as easy as they used to. The clearest example is probably my evening journaling. That used to be something I actually looked forward to, because it helped me wind down and feel like I had put the day away, if that makes sense. Lately, it’s not as smooth or calming. I still try to do it, but sometimes it feels more like another thing I’m working through instead of something restorative.\n\nAnd then mornings affect things too, even if that’s not exactly a social activity. If the morning takes longer than I expected, I can get to school feeling rushed or behind before I’ve even started, and that sort of eats up the energy I’d normally have for the rest of the day. Same with lesson prep — I’m still doing it, but it can feel more drawn out or less efficient.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve dropped hobbies or stopped seeing people because of it. It’s more that the normal routines that help me feel balanced are less relaxing than they used to be, and I feel like I’m carrying that rushed, unsettled feeling into things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on, besides the checking and worries about forgetting something important?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that same kind of doubt, honestly. I don’t know if I’d separate it into a totally different category. The thoughts that keep coming back are things like, “What if I forgot something important?” or “What if I left something in a way that could cause a problem?”\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll get this nagging thought that maybe I left it out wrong or didn’t put it where it should be, even if part of me knows I probably handled it fine. And with school, it can be about whether I set up my classroom materials safely or whether something was left in a way that could be an issue. It’s not like I want to think about it — it just pops back in, and then I feel like I need to do something to make sure.\n\nSo besides that, I don’t think there’s a whole other urge or theme. It’s really the “I may have forgotten something important” feeling, and the doubt doesn’t settle easily even when I try to tell myself I already took care of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is interfering, but maybe not in the way where everything has fallen apart. I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, I’m still taking care of family things, I’m still doing the things I’m supposed to do. So part of me wants to say, “Well, it’s not that bad,” because I am getting through the day.\n\nBut it does take some of the enjoyment or ease out of things. Mornings especially feel more stressful than they should. I’ll get stuck in that checking or second-guessing mode, and then even if I make it to school on time, I feel like I’m arriving already rushed, like I’m behind before the day has even started. And that kind of changes the whole tone of the morning for me.\n\nWith lesson prep, I’m still doing it, but it doesn’t feel as smooth. It feels more effortful, like I’m using extra mental energy just to get to the same place I used to get to more easily. And then by the evening, even things that are supposed to help me unwind, like journaling, don’t feel as restorative as they used to. It’s not that I never enjoy anything, but there’s this extra layer of tension or mental noise over normal daily routines.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s interfering with my enjoyment of daily life. Mildly, maybe, but enough that I notice it and it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are affecting your ability to perform your job as a teacher?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, but not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing my job. I’m showing up, I’m teaching, I’m getting through the day, and I don’t think from the outside someone would necessarily say, “Oh, she can’t function at work.” So I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut it does affect how I feel going into work, especially in the mornings. If I get caught up checking or rechecking things before I leave, then everything takes longer than it should, and I end up arriving at school feeling rushed, like I’m already behind before the students are even there or before the day has really started. And that feeling can stick with me for a while, even if technically I’m on time and nothing has gone wrong.\n\nLesson prep is probably the other part. I’m still preparing, I’m still getting things done, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. It can feel more drawn out, like I have to spend extra mental energy making sure I didn’t miss something or that everything is set up properly. So I can still perform my job, but it feels harder than it should, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I’m more worn out, and even my evening journaling, which used to help me reset, doesn’t feel as restorative. So it’s not like I’m failing at teaching, but the whole process around work feels more stressful and less efficient than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are making it difficult for you to concentrate or be fully present when you're interacting with your students or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, at least sometimes. Not to the point where I can’t teach or I’m not able to interact with people, but there are moments where I’m physically there and doing what I need to do, and mentally there’s still this leftover feeling from the morning, like I’m trying to catch up with myself.\n\nIf I’ve spent too long checking things before leaving, then I get to school feeling rushed or already behind, even if I’m technically there on time. And that makes it harder to feel calm and fully settled when I’m starting the day with the students or talking with colleagues. I can still answer questions and teach and do the normal things, but it feels like I’m using more effort to focus than I should be.\n\nLesson prep is similar. I still get it done, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. I’ll be working through things and it’s like part of my attention is still caught up in making sure I didn’t miss something, so I don’t feel as efficient or as present as I used to.\n\nAnd then by the evening, I notice I’m more mentally tired. Even journaling, which used to help me unwind and feel restored, doesn’t really do that as much lately. So yes, I’d say it affects my concentration and presence, but mildly. I’m still getting through work and family responsibilities — it just takes more out of me than it used to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m still getting through work and family responsibilities — it just takes more out of me than it used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are making it hard for you to enjoy time with friends or family?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 31 / elementary_or_middle_school_teacher / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"I’m still getting through work and family commitments, but my mornings take longer and I sometimes arrive at school feeling rushed or behind before the day even starts. Lesson prep and my evening journaling don’t feel as smooth or restorative as they used to.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it does, but again, it’s not like I’ve stopped seeing people or I’m not doing family things. I’m still getting through my work and family commitments. If there’s something I’m supposed to show up for, I usually do. So in that sense, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely withdrawing or unable to enjoy anyone.

But it does kind of wear me down before I even get to those parts of the day. Like, if my morning takes longer because I’m checking and rechecking, then I arrive at school already feeling rushed or behind, even before the day has really started. And that feeling doesn’t always just disappear. It can make the rest of the day feel like I’m trying to catch up, and then by the time I’m with family later, I’m more mentally tired than I want to be.

And with lesson prep, it’s similar — I still do it, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to, so it takes more out of me. Then in the evening, even journaling, which used to be something that helped me settle and feel more restored, doesn’t quite do that in the same way. So when I’m around friends or family, I can be there and participate, but sometimes I feel like I’m not as relaxed or light as I used to be.

So… yes, mildly. It’s not ruining those relationships, but it does make it harder to fully enjoy the time because I’m carrying that rushed, mentally worn-out feeling with me.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I keep getting stuck on these thoughts that I’ve forgotten something important, even when part of me knows I probably didn’t. Like, I’ll think maybe I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or I didn’t put something away safely in my classroom materials, and then the thought just keeps coming back.\n\nI’m a teacher, so I’m used to being careful and organized, and I know things matter when kids are involved, but this feels different. I’ll try to tell myself, “No, you did that,” but then it’s like, “But what if you didn’t?” And I can’t quite shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over, like checking or cleaning, to try and get rid of these nagging thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. I don’t really have, um, totally separate thoughts that I can think of right now.\n\nIt’s more like, I’ll be pretty sure I handled something, like my aunt’s medicine or setting up things in my classroom, but then the doubt pops back in anyway. Like, “What if I left it out wrong?” or “What if the materials weren’t put somewhere safe?” And even when part of me knows I probably did it correctly, the thought doesn’t just go away. It sort of keeps tapping me on the shoulder, if that makes sense, until I go back over it in my mind or check again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your overall well-being or daily life due to these recurring thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I keep wanting to say it’s “not that bad” because I’m still functioning. It’s not that I never enjoy anything, but there’s this extra layer of tension or mental noise over normal daily routines.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s interfering with my enjoyment of daily life. Mildly, maybe, but enough that I notice it and it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are affecting your ability to perform your job as a teacher?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, but not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing my job. I’ll look over lesson plans or materials, or sometimes I’m just standing there replaying the whole sequence in my head—what I put away, what I closed, what I checked. I know that probably sounds excessive, because I’ve usually already done it, but if I don’t do it a few times, I feel really uncomfortable. Once I’ve gone through it enough, I calm down enough to leave, but sometimes that means I’m running late, which just makes me feel embarrassed and frustrated with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or tense in situations where you're not performing these checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s like the anxiety builds more when I’m trying not to do the checking, if that makes sense. Before I leave the house, I can tell myself, “You already looked at the stove,” or “You already checked the pill organizer,” but then I’ll still feel this tight, uneasy feeling, like I can’t fully trust that it’s done unless I go back over it again.\n\nAnd it happens with my classroom too. I’ll be about to leave and then I start wondering if I put my lesson plans or materials away properly, and even if I’m not physically touching everything again, I’m sort of mentally retracing it—like, “I did this, then I closed that, then I checked that.” I’ll stand there doing that until I feel calm enough to move on.\n\nIt’s not that I want to be late or that I think checking is helpful in the long run. Sometimes I’m aware I’m making myself run late, and that makes me feel embarrassed. But in the moment, if I don’t check or replay it a few times, I feel too tense to just leave.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and checking rituals affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think. Like, I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do—I go to work, I help with family things, I show up for people. So from the outside it may not look like it’s really affecting anything.\n\nBut it does sort of spill over. My mornings take longer than they should because I get stuck checking before I leave, and then I’ll get to school feeling rushed, like I’m already behind before the day even starts. And that can make me a little short or distracted, not because I’m upset with anyone, but because I’m trying to catch up mentally.\n\nWith family, I still keep up with things, but I feel like I’m not as relaxed as I used to be. Even my lesson prep, or journaling in the evening—which used to help me unwind—doesn’t feel as smooth or calming anymore. It’s like there’s this extra layer of tension around getting things “right” or making sure I didn’t miss anything. So relationships haven’t fallen apart or anything, but I can feel it taking up space in my day and affecting how present I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that aren't related to checking or making sure things are done correctly?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s around that same kind of theme, I think—like these nagging thoughts that I forgot something important or didn’t do something safely. I don’t really have, um, totally separate thoughts that I can think of right now.\n\nIt’s more like, I’ll be pretty sure I handled something, like my aunt’s medicine or setting up things in my classroom, but then the doubt pops back in anyway. Like, “What if I left it out wrong?” or “What if the materials weren’t put somewhere safe?” And even when part of me knows I probably did it correctly, the thought doesn’t just go away. It sort of keeps tapping me on the shoulder, if that makes sense, until I go back over it in my mind or check again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your overall well-being or daily life due to these recurring thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I keep wanting to say it’s “not that bad” because I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, I’m still handling family commitments, I’m not missing major responsibilities or anything like that. But it does affect the rhythm of my day more than I’d like to admit.\n\nThe mornings are probably the clearest example. I’ll think I’m ready to leave, and then the checking or the mental reviewing adds these extra minutes that don’t seem like much at first, but they stack up. So by the time I actually get to school, I can feel rushed, like I’m already starting the day behind. And that feeling kind of follows me into the classroom sometimes, even if I’m trying to act normal and get right into teacher mode.\n\nIt’s also made things that used to feel steady or calming feel less smooth. Lesson prep used to be something I could sit down and do in a pretty focused way, but now there’s more tension around whether I’ve done it correctly or put everything where it needs to go. And in the evenings, I journal, which used to help me unwind, but even that doesn’t feel as restorative lately. I’ll still do it, but I don’t always get that same settled feeling from it.\n\nSo I guess overall, I’m managing, but it takes more effort than it used to. It’s like there’s this extra layer of stress built into ordinary parts of the day.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are causing you significant distress or impairment?\n\nPatient: I think the biggest areas are mornings and work, mostly. I wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to function, because I’m not—I do get to work, I do teach, I still handle family commitments. So part of me feels like I shouldn’t call it “significant,” but it is distressing in this quieter, daily kind of way.\n\nLike in the mornings, leaving the house takes longer than it really should. I’ll be basically ready, and then I get pulled into checking or mentally going back over things, and by the time I actually leave, I’m later than I meant to be. Then I get to school feeling rushed or like I’m already behind before the day even starts, which is frustrating because nothing has technically gone wrong yet, but I already feel tense.\n\nAnd lesson prep has changed too. It used to feel more straightforward—like I could sit down, plan, organize things, and feel done. Now it doesn’t feel as smooth, because there’s this extra worry about whether everything is right or whether I missed something. Even my evening journaling, which used to be kind of calming for me, doesn’t feel as restorative as it used to. I still do it, but it doesn’t always help me settle in the same way.\n\nSo yes, I guess it is causing distress and some impairment, just not in a dramatic “my life is falling apart” way. More like it makes ordinary parts of the day take more energy than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of these nagging thoughts or checking rituals?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I really avoid things, not in a clear way. I mean, I still do the things I need to do—I go to work, I get lesson materials ready, I help with family stuff, I leave the house even if it takes me longer than I want. So I wouldn’t say I’m, like, staying away from whole situations because of it.\n\nMaybe there are moments where I feel tempted to put something off because I know it might turn into this whole checking loop, but I usually still do it. It’s more that I move through it with extra hesitation or stress, if that makes sense, rather than avoiding it completely.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure what would count as avoidance. Like, if I take longer to start something because I’m bracing myself for the checking, is that avoidance? Maybe a little? But I haven’t noticed myself outright not doing important tasks or refusing certain situations because of the thoughts. It’s more that I do them, but they feel heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and checking rituals caused problems in your relationships with friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not with friends specifically. At least, I haven’t had anyone say, like, “You’re being distant” or “You’re acting different,” and I’m not aware of any friendships being damaged because of it.\n\nI guess sometimes I might be a little distracted or preoccupied in my own head, but I can’t say that’s caused an actual problem between me and a friend. It’s more something I experience internally, if that makes sense. Like I might feel a little tense or not fully relaxed, but outwardly I’m still showing up and talking to people normally.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really noticed relationship problems with friends because of it. If anything, it’s more just that I’m carrying extra stress around, but I don’t think it’s spilled over into friendships in a major way.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and checking rituals caused problems in your relationships with family members?\n\nPatient: With family, I wouldn’t say it’s caused major conflict or anything like that. I’m still doing the family things I need to do, and I don’t think anyone would say I’ve stopped showing up for them.\n\nBut I do think it affects the “flow” of the day, if that makes sense. Like mornings can take me longer than they should, and if there’s something family-related mixed into that—getting out the door on time, responding to something, helping with whatever needs to happen—I can feel rushed and tense before I’ve even really started the day. Then I arrive at school already feeling behind, and that stress sort of carries over.\n\nI guess at home it’s more that I’m not as relaxed as I want to be. I still keep up with commitments, but I’m using more energy to get through ordinary things. Even in the evenings, journaling used to help me settle down and feel more like myself, and lately it doesn’t feel as smooth or calming. So I wouldn’t say my family relationships are damaged, exactly, but I do think they might be getting a slightly more stressed version of me than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other parts of your life, besides work and family, where you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are causing you a lot of trouble or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… besides work and family, I don’t know that there’s a huge separate area where it’s making me unable to do things. It’s more like it kind of seeps into the parts of the day around everything.\n\nLike mornings are probably the biggest one. I still get up, I still leave, I still get to school, but it takes longer than it should, and then I’ll get there already feeling like I’m behind, even if technically I’m on time or only a little rushed. It puts me in this keyed-up state before the day has even really started.\n\nAnd then with lesson prep, I’m still doing it, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. I might second-guess things or take extra time getting settled into it, so it feels less efficient and more draining than it needs to be.\n\nThe other thing is my evening journaling. That used to be a really calming part of my routine, like a way to end the day and feel organized in my head. Lately it still happens sometimes, but it doesn’t feel as restorative or easy. I don’t know if I’d call that “a lot of trouble,” exactly, but it does bother me because those little routines used to help me feel more balanced. So I guess it’s not stopping my life, but it’s making normal things feel heavier and less smooth.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social or leisure activities that you've stopped doing or are doing less often because of these thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve fully stopped doing social things, no. Like, I’m not canceling plans all the time or avoiding people in a really obvious way. If I have something I’m supposed to do with friends or family, I usually still do it.\n\nBut I do think some of the “leisure” parts of my day don’t feel as easy as they used to. The clearest example is probably my evening journaling. That used to be something I actually looked forward to, because it helped me wind down and feel like I had put the day away, if that makes sense. Lately, it’s not as smooth or calming. I still try to do it, but sometimes it feels more like another thing I’m working through instead of something restorative.\n\nAnd then mornings affect things too, even if that’s not exactly a social activity. If the morning takes longer than I expected, I can get to school feeling rushed or behind before I’ve even started, and that sort of eats up the energy I’d normally have for the rest of the day. Same with lesson prep — I’m still doing it, but it can feel more drawn out or less efficient.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve dropped hobbies or stopped seeing people because of it. It’s more that the normal routines that help me feel balanced are less relaxing than they used to be, and I feel like I’m carrying that rushed, unsettled feeling into things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on, besides the checking and worries about forgetting something important?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that same kind of doubt, honestly. I don’t know if I’d separate it into a totally different category. The thoughts that keep coming back are things like, “What if I forgot something important?” or “What if I left something in a way that could cause a problem?”\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll get this nagging thought that maybe I left it out wrong or didn’t put it where it should be, even if part of me knows I probably handled it fine. And with school, it can be about whether I set up my classroom materials safely or whether something was left in a way that could be an issue. It’s not like I want to think about it — it just pops back in, and then I feel like I need to do something to make sure.\n\nSo besides that, I don’t think there’s a whole other urge or theme. It’s really the “I may have forgotten something important” feeling, and the doubt doesn’t settle easily even when I try to tell myself I already took care of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is interfering, but maybe not in the way where everything has fallen apart. I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, I’m still taking care of family things, I’m still doing the things I’m supposed to do. So part of me wants to say, “Well, it’s not that bad,” because I am getting through the day.\n\nBut it does take some of the enjoyment or ease out of things. Mornings especially feel more stressful than they should. I’ll get stuck in that checking or second-guessing mode, and then even if I make it to school on time, I feel like I’m arriving already rushed, like I’m behind before the day has even started. And that kind of changes the whole tone of the morning for me.\n\nWith lesson prep, I’m still doing it, but it doesn’t feel as smooth. It feels more effortful, like I’m using extra mental energy just to get to the same place I used to get to more easily. And then by the evening, even things that are supposed to help me unwind, like journaling, don’t feel as restorative as they used to. It’s not that I never enjoy anything, but there’s this extra layer of tension or mental noise over normal daily routines.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s interfering with my enjoyment of daily life. Mildly, maybe, but enough that I notice it and it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are affecting your ability to perform your job as a teacher?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, but not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing my job. I’m showing up, I’m teaching, I’m getting through the day, and I don’t think from the outside someone would necessarily say, “Oh, she can’t function at work.” So I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut it does affect how I feel going into work, especially in the mornings. If I get caught up checking or rechecking things before I leave, then everything takes longer than it should, and I end up arriving at school feeling rushed, like I’m already behind before the students are even there or before the day has really started. And that feeling can stick with me for a while, even if technically I’m on time and nothing has gone wrong.\n\nLesson prep is probably the other part. I’m still preparing, I’m still getting things done, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. It can feel more drawn out, like I have to spend extra mental energy making sure I didn’t miss something or that everything is set up properly. So I can still perform my job, but it feels harder than it should, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I’m more worn out, and even my evening journaling, which used to help me reset, doesn’t feel as restorative. So it’s not like I’m failing at teaching, but the whole process around work feels more stressful and less efficient than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are making it difficult for you to concentrate or be fully present when you're interacting with your students or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, at least sometimes. Not to the point where I can’t teach or I’m not able to interact with people, but there are moments where I’m physically there and doing what I need to do, and mentally there’s still this leftover feeling from the morning, like I’m trying to catch up with myself.\n\nIf I’ve spent too long checking things before leaving, then I get to school feeling rushed or already behind, even if I’m technically there on time. And that makes it harder to feel calm and fully settled when I’m starting the day with the students or talking with colleagues. I can still answer questions and teach and do the normal things, but it feels like I’m using more effort to focus than I should be.\n\nLesson prep is similar. I still get it done, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. I’ll be working through things and it’s like part of my attention is still caught up in making sure I didn’t miss something, so I don’t feel as efficient or as present as I used to.\n\nAnd then by the evening, I notice I’m more mentally tired. Even journaling, which used to help me unwind and feel restored, doesn’t really do that as much lately. So yes, I’d say it affects my concentration and presence, but mildly. I’m still getting through work and family responsibilities — it just takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are making it hard for you to enjoy time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, but again, it’s not like I’ve stopped seeing people or I’m not doing family things. I’m still getting through my work and family commitments. If there’s something I’m supposed to show up for, I usually do. So when I’m around friends or family, I can be there and participate, but sometimes I feel like I’m not as relaxed or light as I used to be.\n\nSo… yes, mildly. It’s not ruining those relationships, but it does make it harder to fully enjoy the time because I’m carrying that rushed, mentally worn-out feeling with me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ll be working through things and it’s like part of my attention is still caught up in making sure I didn’t miss something, so I don’t feel as efficient or as present as I used to.\n\nAnd then by the evening, I notice I’m more mentally tired. Even journaling, which used to help me unwind and feel restored, doesn’t really do that as much lately. So yes, I’d say it affects my concentration and presence, but mildly. I’m still getting through work and family responsibilities — it just takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are making it hard for you to enjoy time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, but again, it’s not like I’ve stopped seeing people or I’m not doing family things. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it is, though I keep wanting to qualify that because I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, I’m still teaching, I’m still doing family things, so I don’t want to make it sound like my life has completely fallen apart or anything like that.

But it does affect the quality of my days. The mornings especially feel heavier than they used to. Things that should be simple, like getting out the door, can take longer because I get caught up checking or rechecking, and then even if I make it to school on time, I arrive feeling rushed or behind already. It’s like the day starts with this stress that I didn’t used to have, and then I’m trying to recover from it while also being “on” for my students.

And lesson prep doesn’t feel as smooth anymore either. I still do it, but it feels more effortful, like I’m spending extra mental energy on making sure everything is okay instead of just moving through it. So by the end of the day I feel more drained.

Even in the evenings, when I try to journal — which used to be something that helped me settle and feel restored — it doesn’t always have the same effect now. I can write things down, but I don’t always get that sense of relief or reset from it.

So… yes, I’d say it’s impacting my overall quality of life, but mildly. I’m managing, and from the outside I probably look okay, but internally my days feel more tense and less easy than they used to.

    "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"F42.2\", \"F41.1\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I keep getting stuck on these thoughts that I’ve forgotten something important, even when part of me knows I probably didn’t. Like, I’ll think maybe I left my aunt’s medicine out wrong, or I didn’t put something away safely in my classroom materials, and then the thought just keeps coming back.\n\nI’m a teacher, so I’m used to being careful and organized, and I know things matter when kids are involved, but this feels different. It’s not just “oh, let me double-check.” It’s more like this nagging doubt that keeps popping into my head after I’ve already handled something. I’ll try to tell myself, “No, you did that,” but then it’s like, “But what if you didn’t?” And I can’t quite shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over, like checking or cleaning, to try and get rid of these nagging thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. It’s mostly checking, I think. Like before I leave the house, I’ll go back and look at the same things more than once—the stove, the pill organizer for my aunt, things like that. And at school it’s similar, but with my classroom stuff. I’ll check my lesson plans or materials, especially if I’m worried something could be out of place or not put away right.\n\nSometimes I’m not even physically checking anymore, I’m just standing there silently replaying what I did in my head, like, “Okay, I put that away, then I closed that, then I checked this…” And I’ll do that until I feel calm enough to actually leave. It’s not like I want to spend time doing it. I know it probably looks unnecessary, and part of me knows I already checked, but if I don’t do it, I feel really unsettled.\n\nIt’s not usually hours or anything, but it can make me run late sometimes, which is embarrassing because I’m usually a pretty responsible person. That’s partly why it bothers me so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you're unable to perform these checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not always like a panic feeling, but I get really tense and unsettled if I try to just leave without checking. Like before I leave the house, if I don’t go back over the stove or my aunt’s pill organizer, my mind keeps pulling me back to it. I’ll be halfway to the door and then I’m thinking, “Wait, did I really look? Did I actually make sure?” And then I feel like I can’t move on properly until I check.\n\nIt happens at school too, before I leave my classroom. I’ll look over lesson plans or materials, or sometimes I’m just standing there replaying the whole sequence in my head—what I put away, what I closed, what I checked. I know that probably sounds excessive, because I’ve usually already done it, but if I don’t do it a few times, I feel really uncomfortable. Once I’ve gone through it enough, I calm down enough to leave, but sometimes that means I’m running late, which just makes me feel embarrassed and frustrated with myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or tense in situations where you're not performing these checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s like the anxiety builds more when I’m trying not to do the checking, if that makes sense. Before I leave the house, I can tell myself, “You already looked at the stove,” or “You already checked the pill organizer,” but then I’ll still feel this tight, uneasy feeling, like I can’t fully trust that it’s done unless I go back over it again.\n\nAnd it happens with my classroom too. I’ll be about to leave and then I start wondering if I put my lesson plans or materials away properly, and even if I’m not physically touching everything again, I’m sort of mentally retracing it—like, “I did this, then I closed that, then I checked that.” I’ll stand there doing that until I feel calm enough to move on.\n\nIt’s not that I want to be late or that I think checking is helpful in the long run. Sometimes I’m aware I’m making myself run late, and that makes me feel embarrassed. But in the moment, if I don’t check or replay it a few times, I feel too tense to just leave.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and checking rituals affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Not in a dramatic way, I don’t think. Like, I’m still doing what I’m supposed to do—I go to work, I help with family things, I show up for people. So from the outside it may not look like it’s really affecting anything.\n\nBut it does sort of spill over. My mornings take longer than they should because I get stuck checking before I leave, and then I’ll get to school feeling rushed, like I’m already behind before the day even starts. And that can make me a little short or distracted, not because I’m upset with anyone, but because I’m trying to catch up mentally.\n\nWith family, I still keep up with things, but I feel like I’m not as relaxed as I used to be. Even my lesson prep, or journaling in the evening—which used to help me unwind—doesn’t feel as smooth or calming anymore. It’s like there’s this extra layer of tension around getting things “right” or making sure I didn’t miss anything. So relationships haven’t fallen apart or anything, but I can feel it taking up space in my day and affecting how present I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring, intrusive thoughts that aren't related to checking or making sure things are done correctly?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s around that same kind of theme, I think—like these nagging thoughts that I forgot something important or didn’t do something safely. I don’t really have, um, totally separate thoughts that I can think of right now.\n\nIt’s more like, I’ll be pretty sure I handled something, like my aunt’s medicine or setting up things in my classroom, but then the doubt pops back in anyway. Like, “What if I left it out wrong?” or “What if the materials weren’t put somewhere safe?” And even when part of me knows I probably did it correctly, the thought doesn’t just go away. It sort of keeps tapping me on the shoulder, if that makes sense, until I go back over it in my mind or check again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any impact on your overall well-being or daily life due to these recurring thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I keep wanting to say it’s “not that bad” because I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, I’m still handling family commitments, I’m not missing major responsibilities or anything like that. But it does affect the rhythm of my day more than I’d like to admit.\n\nThe mornings are probably the clearest example. I’ll think I’m ready to leave, and then the checking or the mental reviewing adds these extra minutes that don’t seem like much at first, but they stack up. So by the time I actually get to school, I can feel rushed, like I’m already starting the day behind. And that feeling kind of follows me into the classroom sometimes, even if I’m trying to act normal and get right into teacher mode.\n\nIt’s also made things that used to feel steady or calming feel less smooth. Lesson prep used to be something I could sit down and do in a pretty focused way, but now there’s more tension around whether I’ve done it correctly or put everything where it needs to go. And in the evenings, I journal, which used to help me unwind, but even that doesn’t feel as restorative lately. I’ll still do it, but I don’t always get that same settled feeling from it.\n\nSo I guess overall, I’m managing, but it takes more effort than it used to. It’s like there’s this extra layer of stress built into ordinary parts of the day.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are causing you significant distress or impairment?\n\nPatient: I think the biggest areas are mornings and work, mostly. I wouldn’t say I’m completely unable to function, because I’m not—I do get to work, I do teach, I still handle family commitments. So part of me feels like I shouldn’t call it “significant,” but it is distressing in this quieter, daily kind of way.\n\nLike in the mornings, leaving the house takes longer than it really should. I’ll be basically ready, and then I get pulled into checking or mentally going back over things, and by the time I actually leave, I’m later than I meant to be. Then I get to school feeling rushed or like I’m already behind before the day even starts, which is frustrating because nothing has technically gone wrong yet, but I already feel tense.\n\nAnd lesson prep has changed too. It used to feel more straightforward—like I could sit down, plan, organize things, and feel done. Now it doesn’t feel as smooth, because there’s this extra worry about whether everything is right or whether I missed something. Even my evening journaling, which used to be kind of calming for me, doesn’t feel as restorative as it used to. I still do it, but it doesn’t always help me settle in the same way.\n\nSo yes, I guess it is causing distress and some impairment, just not in a dramatic “my life is falling apart” way. More like it makes ordinary parts of the day take more energy than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or tasks because of these nagging thoughts or checking rituals?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I really avoid things, not in a clear way. I mean, I still do the things I need to do—I go to work, I get lesson materials ready, I help with family stuff, I leave the house even if it takes me longer than I want. So I wouldn’t say I’m, like, staying away from whole situations because of it.\n\nMaybe there are moments where I feel tempted to put something off because I know it might turn into this whole checking loop, but I usually still do it. It’s more that I move through it with extra hesitation or stress, if that makes sense, rather than avoiding it completely.\n\nI guess I’m not totally sure what would count as avoidance. Like, if I take longer to start something because I’m bracing myself for the checking, is that avoidance? Maybe a little? But I haven’t noticed myself outright not doing important tasks or refusing certain situations because of the thoughts. It’s more that I do them, but they feel heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and checking rituals caused problems in your relationships with friends?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not with friends specifically. At least, I haven’t had anyone say, like, “You’re being distant” or “You’re acting different,” and I’m not aware of any friendships being damaged because of it.\n\nI guess sometimes I might be a little distracted or preoccupied in my own head, but I can’t say that’s caused an actual problem between me and a friend. It’s more something I experience internally, if that makes sense. Like I might feel a little tense or not fully relaxed, but outwardly I’m still showing up and talking to people normally.\n\nSo no, I haven’t really noticed relationship problems with friends because of it. If anything, it’s more just that I’m carrying extra stress around, but I don’t think it’s spilled over into friendships in a major way.\n\nDoctor: Have these thoughts and checking rituals caused problems in your relationships with family members?\n\nPatient: With family, I wouldn’t say it’s caused major conflict or anything like that. I’m still doing the family things I need to do, and I don’t think anyone would say I’ve stopped showing up for them.\n\nBut I do think it affects the “flow” of the day, if that makes sense. Like mornings can take me longer than they should, and if there’s something family-related mixed into that—getting out the door on time, responding to something, helping with whatever needs to happen—I can feel rushed and tense before I’ve even really started the day. Then I arrive at school already feeling behind, and that stress sort of carries over.\n\nI guess at home it’s more that I’m not as relaxed as I want to be. I still keep up with commitments, but I’m using more energy to get through ordinary things. Even in the evenings, journaling used to help me settle down and feel more like myself, and lately it doesn’t feel as smooth or calming. So I wouldn’t say my family relationships are damaged, exactly, but I do think they might be getting a slightly more stressed version of me than I’d like.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other parts of your life, besides work and family, where you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are causing you a lot of trouble or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m trying to think… besides work and family, I don’t know that there’s a huge separate area where it’s making me unable to do things. It’s more like it kind of seeps into the parts of the day around everything.\n\nLike mornings are probably the biggest one. I still get up, I still leave, I still get to school, but it takes longer than it should, and then I’ll get there already feeling like I’m behind, even if technically I’m on time or only a little rushed. It puts me in this keyed-up state before the day has even really started.\n\nAnd then with lesson prep, I’m still doing it, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. I might second-guess things or take extra time getting settled into it, so it feels less efficient and more draining than it needs to be.\n\nThe other thing is my evening journaling. That used to be a really calming part of my routine, like a way to end the day and feel organized in my head. Lately it still happens sometimes, but it doesn’t feel as restorative or easy. I don’t know if I’d call that “a lot of trouble,” exactly, but it does bother me because those little routines used to help me feel more balanced. So I guess it’s not stopping my life, but it’s making normal things feel heavier and less smooth.\n\nDoctor: Are there any social or leisure activities that you've stopped doing or are doing less often because of these thoughts and checking rituals?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve fully stopped doing social things, no. Like, I’m not canceling plans all the time or avoiding people in a really obvious way. If I have something I’m supposed to do with friends or family, I usually still do it.\n\nBut I do think some of the “leisure” parts of my day don’t feel as easy as they used to. The clearest example is probably my evening journaling. That used to be something I actually looked forward to, because it helped me wind down and feel like I had put the day away, if that makes sense. Lately, it’s not as smooth or calming. I still try to do it, but sometimes it feels more like another thing I’m working through instead of something restorative.\n\nAnd then mornings affect things too, even if that’s not exactly a social activity. If the morning takes longer than I expected, I can get to school feeling rushed or behind before I’ve even started, and that sort of eats up the energy I’d normally have for the rest of the day. Same with lesson prep — I’m still doing it, but it can feel more drawn out or less efficient.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve dropped hobbies or stopped seeing people because of it. It’s more that the normal routines that help me feel balanced are less relaxing than they used to be, and I feel like I’m carrying that rushed, unsettled feeling into things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other recurring thoughts or urges that you feel compelled to act on, besides the checking and worries about forgetting something important?\n\nPatient: Mostly it’s that same kind of doubt, honestly. I don’t know if I’d separate it into a totally different category. The thoughts that keep coming back are things like, “What if I forgot something important?” or “What if I left something in a way that could cause a problem?”\n\nLike with my aunt’s medicine, I’ll get this nagging thought that maybe I left it out wrong or didn’t put it where it should be, even if part of me knows I probably handled it fine. And with school, it can be about whether I set up my classroom materials safely or whether something was left in a way that could be an issue. It’s not like I want to think about it — it just pops back in, and then I feel like I need to do something to make sure.\n\nSo besides that, I don’t think there’s a whole other urge or theme. It’s really the “I may have forgotten something important” feeling, and the doubt doesn’t settle easily even when I try to tell myself I already took care of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is interfering, but maybe not in the way where everything has fallen apart. I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, I’m still taking care of family things, I’m still doing the things I’m supposed to do. So part of me wants to say, “Well, it’s not that bad,” because I am getting through the day.\n\nBut it does take some of the enjoyment or ease out of things. Mornings especially feel more stressful than they should. I’ll get stuck in that checking or second-guessing mode, and then even if I make it to school on time, I feel like I’m arriving already rushed, like I’m behind before the day has even started. And that kind of changes the whole tone of the morning for me.\n\nWith lesson prep, I’m still doing it, but it doesn’t feel as smooth. It feels more effortful, like I’m using extra mental energy just to get to the same place I used to get to more easily. And then by the evening, even things that are supposed to help me unwind, like journaling, don’t feel as restorative as they used to. It’s not that I never enjoy anything, but there’s this extra layer of tension or mental noise over normal daily routines.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it’s interfering with my enjoyment of daily life. Mildly, maybe, but enough that I notice it and it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are affecting your ability to perform your job as a teacher?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, but not in a dramatic way. I’m still doing my job. I’m showing up, I’m teaching, I’m getting through the day, and I don’t think from the outside someone would necessarily say, “Oh, she can’t function at work.” So I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nBut it does affect how I feel going into work, especially in the mornings. If I get caught up checking or rechecking things before I leave, then everything takes longer than it should, and I end up arriving at school feeling rushed, like I’m already behind before the students are even there or before the day has really started. And that feeling can stick with me for a while, even if technically I’m on time and nothing has gone wrong.\n\nLesson prep is probably the other part. I’m still preparing, I’m still getting things done, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. It can feel more drawn out, like I have to spend extra mental energy making sure I didn’t miss something or that everything is set up properly. So I can still perform my job, but it feels harder than it should, if that makes sense.\n\nAnd then by the time I get home, I’m more worn out, and even my evening journaling, which used to help me reset, doesn’t feel as restorative. So it’s not like I’m failing at teaching, but the whole process around work feels more stressful and less efficient than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are making it difficult for you to concentrate or be fully present when you're interacting with your students or colleagues?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it does, at least sometimes. Not to the point where I can’t teach or I’m not able to interact with people, but there are moments where I’m physically there and doing what I need to do, and mentally there’s still this leftover feeling from the morning, like I’m trying to catch up with myself.\n\nIf I’ve spent too long checking things before leaving, then I get to school feeling rushed or already behind, even if I’m technically there on time. And that makes it harder to feel calm and fully settled when I’m starting the day with the students or talking with colleagues. I can still answer questions and teach and do the normal things, but it feels like I’m using more effort to focus than I should be.\n\nLesson prep is similar. I still get it done, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to. I’ll be working through things and it’s like part of my attention is still caught up in making sure I didn’t miss something, so I don’t feel as efficient or as present as I used to.\n\nAnd then by the evening, I notice I’m more mentally tired. Even journaling, which used to help me unwind and feel restored, doesn’t really do that as much lately. So yes, I’d say it affects my concentration and presence, but mildly. I’m still getting through work and family responsibilities — it just takes more out of me than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are making it hard for you to enjoy time with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, but again, it’s not like I’ve stopped seeing people or I’m not doing family things. I’m still getting through my work and family commitments. If there’s something I’m supposed to show up for, I usually do. So in that sense, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely withdrawing or unable to enjoy anyone.\n\nBut it does kind of wear me down before I even get to those parts of the day. Like, if my morning takes longer because I’m checking and rechecking, then I arrive at school already feeling rushed or behind, even before the day has really started. And that feeling doesn’t always just disappear. It can make the rest of the day feel like I’m trying to catch up, and then by the time I’m with family later, I’m more mentally tired than I want to be.\n\nAnd with lesson prep, it’s similar — I still do it, but it doesn’t feel as smooth as it used to, so it takes more out of me. Then in the evening, even journaling, which used to be something that helped me settle and feel more restored, doesn’t quite do that in the same way. So when I’m around friends or family, I can be there and participate, but sometimes I feel like I’m not as relaxed or light as I used to be.\n\nSo… yes, mildly. It’s not ruining those relationships, but it does make it harder to fully enjoy the time because I’m carrying that rushed, mentally worn-out feeling with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these thoughts and checking rituals are significantly impacting your overall quality of life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is, though I keep wanting to qualify that because I’m still functioning. I’m still going to work, I’m still teaching, I’m still doing family things, so I don’t want to make it sound like my life has completely fallen apart or anything like that.\n\nBut it does affect the quality of my days. The mornings especially feel heavier than they used to. Things that should be simple, like getting out the door, can take longer because I get caught up checking or rechecking, and then even if I make it to school on time, I arrive feeling rushed or behind already. It’s like the day starts with this stress that I didn’t used to have, and then I’m trying to recover from it while also being “on” for my students.\n\nAnd lesson prep doesn’t feel as smooth anymore either. I still do it, but it feels more effortful, like I’m spending extra mental energy on making sure everything is okay instead of just moving through it. So by the end of the day I feel more drained.\n\nEven in the evenings, when I try to journal — which used to be something that helped me settle and feel restored — it doesn’t always have the same effect now. I can write things down, but I don’t always get that sense of relief or reset from it.\n\nSo… yes, I’d say it’s impacting my overall quality of life, but mildly. I’m managing, and from the outside I probably look okay, but internally my days feel more tense and less easy than they used to."
